Yeah… yeah, I have. Stuff I used to look forward to just feels like one more thing I’m supposed to do.

Like fishing used to calm me down — even if I didn’t catch anything, just being out there was enough. Now I think about getting the gear together and I’m already tired of it. Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say every single thing, but it’s pretty much the stuff that usually keeps me level, you know?

So… yeah, most days I just don’t really care about those things the way I used to.

I’m not sure. I mean, simple tasks… depends what you mean by simple, I guess.

Like, at work, if I’ve got to restock the little batteries by the register, that always feels like it takes forever because they put every size in packaging that looks almost the same. Whoever designs those things ought to be made to stock them for eight hours, honestly.

I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. I still don’t really know how to answer it.

I don’t feel like I’m walking in slow motion or anything. And I’m not, like, bouncing off the walls either. No, I don’t think anybody’s said that to me. Not in those words.

People comment on all kinds of things at work, you know—“you look tired,” or “can you open another register,” or they ask where the lightbulbs are when they’re standing right next to them. But nobody’s told me I’m talking weird or moving strange or anything like that.

My mother was like that, she’d say, “Well, you seem… different,” and then you’d have to figure out what crime you committed. It’s just been sitting in my head.\n\nAnd, you know, I almost didn’t come because I had to switch shifts at the store, and my manager’s been on me about that, but… yeah. That’s the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have these thoughts been happening to you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I don’t know, recently enough that it started to bother me. I keep catching myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Not like I’ve made some plan to hurt myself or anything.\n\nI mean, mornings are hard anyway because my schedule at the store changes all the time, and then I’m lying there thinking about work and bills, and that thought just kind of shows up.\n\nDoctor: When did these thoughts first start? Was it within the past two weeks, or have they been happening for longer than that?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been longer than just two weeks… I can’t pin down the first day, exactly. It’s more like I keep catching myself thinking it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up, and then I try to push it away and get on with work or whatever.\n\nI haven’t made any plan to hurt myself, though. It’s not like that. It just keeps coming back, and that’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there was a time before these thoughts started where you felt significantly better, or has this been going on without a break?\n\nPatient: There was… yeah, there was a before, I guess. I mean, I wasn’t exactly winning any awards for happiness or anything, but I was getting through better than this.\n\nThen after that crash, it’s like this low spell just kind of settled in and never really lifted. It’s been hanging around about three months now, I think. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like work stuff, because retail around any busy season will grind you down, you know, people arguing over returns and coupons… but no, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once liked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. Stuff I used to look forward to just feels like one more thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nLike fishing used to calm me down — even if I didn’t catch anything, just being out there was enough. Now I think about getting the gear together and I’m already tired of it. Same with going through my vinyl records, or tailgating with people, even checking on the home-brew I’ve got going… it all just feels like chores now.\n\nAnd I used to be the guy who’d talk your ear off about an old record pressing or which cooler keeps ice longest, you know? Now I just kind of look at it and don’t care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, even if you haven’t been doing much, or that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally could handle?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yeah, maybe, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s “more than usual,” you know? I mean, I’m 54 and I’m on my feet at the store all day, and half the customers act like I personally wrote the return policy just to ruin their afternoon.\n\nSo I come home worn out, but I’ve always kind of been worn out after work. I can’t say I’ve really tracked it or noticed some big change. It’s more like… some days I just don’t feel like doing much, but whether that’s energy or just not wanting to deal with anything, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you experience little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just some, and that this has been happening most days, all day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say every single thing, but it’s pretty much the stuff that usually keeps me level, you know?\n\nFishing, going through my records, tailgating, messing with the home-brew — all that used to be something I’d look forward to. Now it’s like, I look at the fishing gear or the records and it just feels like another chore. Like when you’ve got a list on the fridge and you keep walking past it pretending you didn’t see it.\n\nSo… yeah, most days I just don’t really care about those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… after that whole burst of energy kind of wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table with my coffee, and I wasn’t even really drinking it half the time, just staring at it. Felt… empty, I guess. And ashamed, too. Like I’d made a mess of things somehow, even if I couldn’t put my finger on exactly what.\n\nMost of the day? Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I don’t really sit down and count calories or anything — my ex used to do all that kind of stuff, drove me nuts with the little notebook — but lately supper will just be sitting there and it feels like too much work to even deal with it.\n\nMost days I have to remind myself to eat something. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. So… yeah, appetite’s been off, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I feel like I’m dragging, but that could just be, you know, getting older or being on my feet at the store all day. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. Nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. More ashamed than anything.\n\nLike after that big rush of energy finally wore off, it was like someone just pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee going cold, just staring at it like it had answers or something. My dad used to do that with his cigarettes at breakfast, just sit and stare out the window, and I remember thinking, “What’s his problem?” Funny how you turn into people.\n\nBut yeah… I felt empty. And ashamed. Like I’d done something wrong, even if I couldn’t really point to what exactly. So, no clear reason, maybe, but it still sits there on you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly than usual, or that even simple tasks feel like they take more effort to complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, simple tasks… depends what you mean by simple, I guess.\n\nLike, at work, if I’ve got to restock the little batteries by the register, that always feels like it takes forever because they put every size in packaging that looks almost the same. Whoever designs those things ought to be made to stock them for eight hours, honestly.\n\nBut thoughts moving slower? I don’t know if I’d say that. Sometimes I just sit there a minute before doing something, but I don’t know if that’s my thoughts or just being tired or not wanting to deal with it. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. I still don’t really know how to answer it.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m walking in slow motion or anything. And I’m not, like, bouncing off the walls either. I mean, sometimes I shift around if a chair’s uncomfortable — my brother had this recliner that looked nice but it was awful, like sitting on a sack of rocks — but that’s not really what you’re asking, I guess.\n\nTalking slower… nobody’s told me that. At work people usually just want me to hurry up and find the price on something, so I don’t think they’re paying attention to how I sound. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or restless, or have you been told that your movements or speech seem unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anybody’s said that to me. I don’t know if I’d call it “nearly every day.” I get tired, sure. Everybody gets tired. Standing at a register under those fluorescent lights, that’ll drain anybody, even if you’re not lifting boxes or anything.

But a noticeable loss of energy… I’m not sure. Some days I come home and just sit down longer than I meant to, and then the TV’s on some cooking show I didn’t even choose. But I don’t really track whether that’s fatigue or just, you know, being done with people for the day.

So… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. Not like I can say, “yes, every day I’m exhausted.”

Felt… empty, I guess. And ashamed, too. Like I’d made a mess of things somehow, even if I couldn’t put my finger on exactly what.\n\nMost of the day? Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I don’t really sit down and count calories or anything — my ex used to do all that kind of stuff, drove me nuts with the little notebook — but lately supper will just be sitting there and it feels like too much work to even deal with it.\n\nMost days I have to remind myself to eat something. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. So… yeah, appetite’s been off, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I feel like I’m dragging, but that could just be, you know, getting older or being on my feet at the store all day. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. Nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. More ashamed than anything.\n\nLike after that big rush of energy finally wore off, it was like someone just pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee going cold, just staring at it like it had answers or something. My dad used to do that with his cigarettes at breakfast, just sit and stare out the window, and I remember thinking, “What’s his problem?” Funny how you turn into people.\n\nBut yeah… I felt empty. And ashamed. Like I’d done something wrong, even if I couldn’t really point to what exactly. So, no clear reason, maybe, but it still sits there on you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly than usual, or that even simple tasks feel like they take more effort to complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, simple tasks… depends what you mean by simple, I guess.\n\nLike, at work, if I’ve got to restock the little batteries by the register, that always feels like it takes forever because they put every size in packaging that looks almost the same. Whoever designs those things ought to be made to stock them for eight hours, honestly.\n\nBut thoughts moving slower? I don’t know if I’d say that. Sometimes I just sit there a minute before doing something, but I don’t know if that’s my thoughts or just being tired or not wanting to deal with it. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. I still don’t really know how to answer it.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m walking in slow motion or anything. And I’m not, like, bouncing off the walls either. I mean, sometimes I shift around if a chair’s uncomfortable — my brother had this recliner that looked nice but it was awful, like sitting on a sack of rocks — but that’s not really what you’re asking, I guess.\n\nTalking slower… nobody’s told me that. At work people usually just want me to hurry up and find the price on something, so I don’t think they’re paying attention to how I sound. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or restless, or have you been told that your movements or speech seem unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anybody’s said that to me. Not in those words.\n\nPeople comment on all kinds of things at work, you know—“you look tired,” or “can you open another register,” or they ask where the lightbulbs are when they’re standing right next to them. But nobody’s told me I’m talking weird or moving strange or anything like that.\n\nIf they did, maybe I missed it. Sometimes people hint at things instead of saying them straight. My mother was like that, she’d say, “Well, you seem… different,” and then you’d have to figure out what crime you committed. But no, I can’t think of anyone actually saying I seemed slowed down or restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “nearly every day.” I get tired, sure. Everybody gets tired. Standing at a register under those fluorescent lights, that’ll drain anybody, even if you’re not lifting boxes or anything.\n\nBut a noticeable loss of energy… I’m not sure. Some days I come home and just sit down longer than I meant to, and then the TV’s on some cooking show I didn’t even choose. But I don’t really track whether that’s fatigue or just, you know, being done with people for the day.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. Not like I can say, “yes, every day I’m exhausted.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But I don’t really track whether that’s fatigue or just, you know, being done with people for the day.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. Not like I can say, “yes, every day I’m exhausted.”\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt down or depressed most of the time for at least two years, even if there were periods when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"After that burst of energy wore off, I felt like somebody had pulled the plug on me, and I would sit at the table staring at my coffee feeling empty and ashamed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two years… I don’t know how to measure it like that. I don’t keep a calendar of moods, you know? I’ve got one of those free bank calendars on the fridge, but it’s mostly got dentist appointments from three months ago still circled.

But after that burst of energy wore off, yeah… I felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee, and it would get cold, and I’d just feel empty. Ashamed, too, though I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed of exactly.

Whether that was most of the time for two whole years… I’m not sure I can say it that neatly. But there were stretches where it felt pretty heavy.

It’s not like that. It just keeps coming back, and that’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there was a time before these thoughts started where you felt significantly better, or has this been going on without a break?\n\nPatient: There was… yeah, there was a before, I guess. Now I think about getting the gear together and I’m already tired of it. Same with going through my vinyl records, or tailgating with people, even checking on the home-brew I’ve got going… it all just feels like chores now.\n\nAnd I used to be the guy who’d talk your ear off about an old record pressing or which cooler keeps ice longest, you know? Now I just kind of look at it and don’t care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, even if you haven’t been doing much, or that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally could handle?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yeah, maybe, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s “more than usual,” you know? I mean, I’m 54 and I’m on my feet at the store all day, and half the customers act like I personally wrote the return policy just to ruin their afternoon.\n\nSo I come home worn out, but I’ve always kind of been worn out after work. I can’t say I’ve really tracked it or noticed some big change. It’s more like… some days I just don’t feel like doing much, but whether that’s energy or just not wanting to deal with anything, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you experience little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just some, and that this has been happening most days, all day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say every single thing, but it’s pretty much the stuff that usually keeps me level, you know?\n\nFishing, going through my records, tailgating, messing with the home-brew — all that used to be something I’d look forward to. Now it’s like, I look at the fishing gear or the records and it just feels like another chore. Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I don’t really sit down and count calories or anything — my ex used to do all that kind of stuff, drove me nuts with the little notebook — but lately supper will just be sitting there and it feels like too much work to even deal with it.\n\nMost days I have to remind myself to eat something. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. So… yeah, appetite’s been off, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I feel like I’m dragging, but that could just be, you know, getting older or being on my feet at the store all day. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. Nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. More ashamed than anything.\n\nLike after that big rush of energy finally wore off, it was like someone just pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee going cold, just staring at it like it had answers or something. Sometimes I just sit there a minute before doing something, but I don’t know if that’s my thoughts or just being tired or not wanting to deal with it. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. Most days over two years… I don’t know, that’s the part I trip over. I’m not good at lining it up like that.

But after that big burst of energy ended, yeah, I felt like somebody had just pulled the plug on me. So was it most days? It felt like a lot of days. I can say that much.

Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. Same with going through my vinyl records, or tailgating with people, even checking on the home-brew I’ve got going… it all just feels like chores now.\n\nAnd I used to be the guy who’d talk your ear off about an old record pressing or which cooler keeps ice longest, you know? Now I just kind of look at it and don’t care.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, even if you haven’t been doing much, or that you don’t have the energy to do things you normally could handle?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, yeah, maybe, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s “more than usual,” you know? I mean, I’m 54 and I’m on my feet at the store all day, and half the customers act like I personally wrote the return policy just to ruin their afternoon.\n\nSo I come home worn out, but I’ve always kind of been worn out after work. I can’t say I’ve really tracked it or noticed some big change. It’s more like… some days I just don’t feel like doing much, but whether that’s energy or just not wanting to deal with anything, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you experience little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just some, and that this has been happening most days, all day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say every single thing, but it’s pretty much the stuff that usually keeps me level, you know?\n\nFishing, going through my records, tailgating, messing with the home-brew — all that used to be something I’d look forward to. Now it’s like, I look at the fishing gear or the records and it just feels like another chore. Like when you’ve got a list on the fridge and you keep walking past it pretending you didn’t see it.\n\nSo… yeah, most days I just don’t really care about those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… after that whole burst of energy kind of wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table with my coffee, and I wasn’t even really drinking it half the time, just staring at it. Felt… empty, I guess. And ashamed, too. Like I’d made a mess of things somehow, even if I couldn’t put my finger on exactly what.\n\nMost of the day? Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I don’t really sit down and count calories or anything — my ex used to do all that kind of stuff, drove me nuts with the little notebook — but lately supper will just be sitting there and it feels like too much work to even deal with it.\n\nMost days I have to remind myself to eat something. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. So… yeah, appetite’s been off, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I feel like I’m dragging, but that could just be, you know, getting older or being on my feet at the store all day. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. Nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. More ashamed than anything.\n\nLike after that big rush of energy finally wore off, it was like someone just pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee going cold, just staring at it like it had answers or something. My dad used to do that with his cigarettes at breakfast, just sit and stare out the window, and I remember thinking, “What’s his problem?” Funny how you turn into people.\n\nBut yeah… I felt empty. And ashamed. Like I’d done something wrong, even if I couldn’t really point to what exactly. So, no clear reason, maybe, but it still sits there on you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly than usual, or that even simple tasks feel like they take more effort to complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, simple tasks… depends what you mean by simple, I guess.\n\nLike, at work, if I’ve got to restock the little batteries by the register, that always feels like it takes forever because they put every size in packaging that looks almost the same. Whoever designs those things ought to be made to stock them for eight hours, honestly.\n\nBut thoughts moving slower? I don’t know if I’d say that. Sometimes I just sit there a minute before doing something, but I don’t know if that’s my thoughts or just being tired or not wanting to deal with it. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. I still don’t really know how to answer it.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m walking in slow motion or anything. And I’m not, like, bouncing off the walls either. I mean, sometimes I shift around if a chair’s uncomfortable — my brother had this recliner that looked nice but it was awful, like sitting on a sack of rocks — but that’s not really what you’re asking, I guess.\n\nTalking slower… nobody’s told me that. At work people usually just want me to hurry up and find the price on something, so I don’t think they’re paying attention to how I sound. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or restless, or have you been told that your movements or speech seem unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anybody’s said that to me. Not in those words.\n\nPeople comment on all kinds of things at work, you know—“you look tired,” or “can you open another register,” or they ask where the lightbulbs are when they’re standing right next to them. But nobody’s told me I’m talking weird or moving strange or anything like that.\n\nIf they did, maybe I missed it. Sometimes people hint at things instead of saying them straight. My mother was like that, she’d say, “Well, you seem… different,” and then you’d have to figure out what crime you committed. But no, I can’t think of anyone actually saying I seemed slowed down or restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “nearly every day.” I get tired, sure. Everybody gets tired. Standing at a register under those fluorescent lights, that’ll drain anybody, even if you’re not lifting boxes or anything.\n\nBut a noticeable loss of energy… I’m not sure. Some days I come home and just sit down longer than I meant to, and then the TV’s on some cooking show I didn’t even choose. But I don’t really track whether that’s fatigue or just, you know, being done with people for the day.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. Not like I can say, “yes, every day I’m exhausted.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the time for at least two years, even if there were periods when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: Two years… I don’t know how to measure it like that. I don’t keep a calendar of moods, you know? I’ve got one of those free bank calendars on the fridge, but it’s mostly got dentist appointments from three months ago still circled.\n\nBut after that burst of energy wore off, yeah… I felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee, and it would get cold, and I’d just feel empty. Ashamed, too, though I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed of exactly.\n\nWhether that was most of the time for two whole years… I’m not sure I can say it that neatly. But there were stretches where it felt pretty heavy.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed for most days over the past two years, even if there were times when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: Most days over two years… I don’t know, that’s the part I trip over. I’d sit at the table with my coffee and just stare at it until it went cold, feeling empty… and ashamed, too. Funny, because sometimes I couldn’t even tell you what I’d done wrong. It’s more like… some days I just don’t feel like doing much, but whether that’s energy or just not wanting to deal with anything, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you experience little to no interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just some, and that this has been happening most days, all day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say every single thing, but it’s pretty much the stuff that usually keeps me level, you know?\n\nFishing, going through my records, tailgating, messing with the home-brew — all that used to be something I’d look forward to. Now it’s like, I look at the fishing gear or the records and it just feels like another chore. Like when you’ve got a list on the fridge and you keep walking past it pretending you didn’t see it.\n\nSo… yeah, most days I just don’t really care about those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… after that whole burst of energy kind of wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table with my coffee, and I wasn’t even really drinking it half the time, just staring at it. Felt… empty, I guess. And ashamed, too. Like I’d made a mess of things somehow, even if I couldn’t put my finger on exactly what.\n\nMost of the day? Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I don’t really sit down and count calories or anything — my ex used to do all that kind of stuff, drove me nuts with the little notebook — but lately supper will just be sitting there and it feels like too much work to even deal with it.\n\nMost days I have to remind myself to eat something. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. So… yeah, appetite’s been off, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I feel like I’m dragging, but that could just be, you know, getting older or being on my feet at the store all day. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. Nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. More ashamed than anything.\n\nLike after that big rush of energy finally wore off, it was like someone just pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee going cold, just staring at it like it had answers or something. My dad used to do that with his cigarettes at breakfast, just sit and stare out the window, and I remember thinking, “What’s his problem?” Funny how you turn into people.\n\nBut yeah… I felt empty. And ashamed. Like I’d done something wrong, even if I couldn’t really point to what exactly. So, no clear reason, maybe, but it still sits there on you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly than usual, or that even simple tasks feel like they take more effort to complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, simple tasks… depends what you mean by simple, I guess.\n\nLike, at work, if I’ve got to restock the little batteries by the register, that always feels like it takes forever because they put every size in packaging that looks almost the same. Whoever designs those things ought to be made to stock them for eight hours, honestly.\n\nBut thoughts moving slower? I don’t know if I’d say that. Sometimes I just sit there a minute before doing something, but I don’t know if that’s my thoughts or just being tired or not wanting to deal with it. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. I still don’t really know how to answer it.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m walking in slow motion or anything. And I’m not, like, bouncing off the walls either. I mean, sometimes I shift around if a chair’s uncomfortable — my brother had this recliner that looked nice but it was awful, like sitting on a sack of rocks — but that’s not really what you’re asking, I guess.\n\nTalking slower… nobody’s told me that. At work people usually just want me to hurry up and find the price on something, so I don’t think they’re paying attention to how I sound. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or restless, or have you been told that your movements or speech seem unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anybody’s said that to me. Not in those words.\n\nPeople comment on all kinds of things at work, you know—“you look tired,” or “can you open another register,” or they ask where the lightbulbs are when they’re standing right next to them. But nobody’s told me I’m talking weird or moving strange or anything like that.\n\nIf they did, maybe I missed it. Sometimes people hint at things instead of saying them straight. My mother was like that, she’d say, “Well, you seem… different,” and then you’d have to figure out what crime you committed. But no, I can’t think of anyone actually saying I seemed slowed down or restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “nearly every day.” I get tired, sure. I’ve got one of those free bank calendars on the fridge, but it’s mostly got dentist appointments from three months ago still circled.\n\nBut after that burst of energy wore off, yeah… I felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee, and it would get cold, and I’d just feel empty. Ashamed, too, though I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed of exactly.\n\nWhether that was most of the time for two whole years… I’m not sure I can say it that neatly. But there were stretches where it felt pretty heavy.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed for most days over the past two years, even if there were times when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: Most days over two years… I don’t know, that’s the part I trip over. Yeah… I mean, it’s hard for me to put a neat “two years” label on it, but after that burst of energy wore off, I really did feel like somebody pulled the plug on me.

Like when you’ve got a list on the fridge and you keep walking past it pretending you didn’t see it.\n\nSo… yeah, most days I just don’t really care about those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… after that whole burst of energy kind of wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d just sit at the kitchen table with my coffee, and I wasn’t even really drinking it half the time, just staring at it. Felt… empty, I guess. And ashamed, too. Like I’d made a mess of things somehow, even if I couldn’t put my finger on exactly what.\n\nMost of the day? Yeah, a lot of days it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I don’t really sit down and count calories or anything — my ex used to do all that kind of stuff, drove me nuts with the little notebook — but lately supper will just be sitting there and it feels like too much work to even deal with it.\n\nMost days I have to remind myself to eat something. And my jeans are definitely looser, which I noticed because I keep having to hitch them up at work, and the belt I usually use is on a different notch now. So… yeah, appetite’s been off, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed that exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I feel like I’m dragging, but that could just be, you know, getting older or being on my feet at the store all day. Retail floors are murder on your knees — and they changed the mats behind the counter a while back, which I still think was a mistake, but nobody asks me.\n\nAs far as talking slower or pacing around… I’m not sure. Nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least. I guess I’d have to think on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a burden, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess that’s close to it. More ashamed than anything.\n\nLike after that big rush of energy finally wore off, it was like someone just pulled the plug out of the wall. I’d sit there at the kitchen table with my coffee going cold, just staring at it like it had answers or something. My dad used to do that with his cigarettes at breakfast, just sit and stare out the window, and I remember thinking, “What’s his problem?” Funny how you turn into people.\n\nBut yeah… I felt empty. And ashamed. Like I’d done something wrong, even if I couldn’t really point to what exactly. So, no clear reason, maybe, but it still sits there on you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly than usual, or that even simple tasks feel like they take more effort to complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, simple tasks… depends what you mean by simple, I guess.\n\nLike, at work, if I’ve got to restock the little batteries by the register, that always feels like it takes forever because they put every size in packaging that looks almost the same. Whoever designs those things ought to be made to stock them for eight hours, honestly.\n\nBut thoughts moving slower? I don’t know if I’d say that. Sometimes I just sit there a minute before doing something, but I don’t know if that’s my thoughts or just being tired or not wanting to deal with it. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me that one already… or something like it. I still don’t really know how to answer it.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m walking in slow motion or anything. And I’m not, like, bouncing off the walls either. I mean, sometimes I shift around if a chair’s uncomfortable — my brother had this recliner that looked nice but it was awful, like sitting on a sack of rocks — but that’s not really what you’re asking, I guess.\n\nTalking slower… nobody’s told me that. At work people usually just want me to hurry up and find the price on something, so I don’t think they’re paying attention to how I sound. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or restless, or have you been told that your movements or speech seem unusual to other people?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anybody’s said that to me. Not in those words.\n\nPeople comment on all kinds of things at work, you know—“you look tired,” or “can you open another register,” or they ask where the lightbulbs are when they’re standing right next to them. But nobody’s told me I’m talking weird or moving strange or anything like that.\n\nIf they did, maybe I missed it. Sometimes people hint at things instead of saying them straight. My mother was like that, she’d say, “Well, you seem… different,” and then you’d have to figure out what crime you committed. But no, I can’t think of anyone actually saying I seemed slowed down or restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “nearly every day.” I get tired, sure. Everybody gets tired. Standing at a register under those fluorescent lights, that’ll drain anybody, even if you’re not lifting boxes or anything.\n\nBut a noticeable loss of energy… I’m not sure. Some days I come home and just sit down longer than I meant to, and then the TV’s on some cooking show I didn’t even choose. But I don’t really track whether that’s fatigue or just, you know, being done with people for the day.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a clear way. Not like I can say, “yes, every day I’m exhausted.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the time for at least two years, even if there were periods when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: Two years… I don’t know how to measure it like that. I don’t keep a calendar of moods, you know? I’ve got one of those free bank calendars on the fridge, but it’s mostly got dentist appointments from three months ago still circled.\n\nBut after that burst of energy wore off, yeah… I felt like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee, and it would get cold, and I’d just feel empty. Ashamed, too, though I couldn’t always tell you what I was ashamed of exactly.\n\nWhether that was most of the time for two whole years… I’m not sure I can say it that neatly. But there were stretches where it felt pretty heavy.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed for most days over the past two years, even if there were times when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: Most days over two years… I don’t know, that’s the part I trip over. I’m not good at lining it up like that.\n\nBut after that big burst of energy ended, yeah, I felt like somebody had just pulled the plug on me. I’d sit at the table with my coffee and just stare at it until it went cold, feeling empty… and ashamed, too. Funny, because sometimes I couldn’t even tell you what I’d done wrong. It’s like when you leave the house and think you forgot to lock the door, but it’s inside your chest instead.\n\nSo was it most days? It felt like a lot of days. I can say that much.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed for most of the time over the past two years, even if there were times when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I think so, at least for a lot of it. After that burst of energy wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee, and it would just sit there getting cold. I’d feel empty, and ashamed too, which is strange because sometimes I couldn’t even tell you what I was ashamed of. Like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there—only it’s a bad feeling instead of just annoying.\n\nSo… most of the time? I don’t know how to pin it down exactly, but it felt like that was around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have felt down or depressed for most of the time over the past two years, even if there were times when it was a little better or a little worse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard for me to put a neat “two years” label on it, but after that burst of energy wore off, I really did feel like somebody pulled the plug on me.\n\nI’d sit at the table with my coffee and just stare at it, and the coffee would get cold. My wife used to say I made coffee just to ignore it—well, not used to, I mean, she’d joke like that sometimes. But I’d just sit there feeling empty… and ashamed, too, even when I couldn’t point to what I was ashamed about.\n\nSo, most of the time? I guess I’d say it was around a lot, yeah."
